The Riemann-Stieltjes integral over the deterministic functions does not depend on the particular choice of the point $\tau _ { \mathrm { i } }$ within the intervals $[ \mathrm { t } _ { \mathrm { i } - 1 } , \ \mathrm { t } _ { \mathrm { i } } ]$ . However, if the function f(t) is random, the sum $\mathbf { S } _ { \mathrm { n } }$ does depend on the choice of $\tau _ { \mathrm { i } }$ . Consider, for example, the sum (4.4.4) for the case $\mathrm { { f ( t ) } = \mathrm { { g ( t ) } = W ( t ) } }$ (where W(t) is the Wiener process). It follows from (4.1.16) that 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} \mathrm{E} [ \mathrm{S} _ {\mathrm{n}} ] & = \mathrm{E} \left[ \sum_ {\mathrm{i} = 1} ^ {\mathrm{n}} \mathrm{W} (\tau_ {\mathrm{i}}) \{\mathrm{W} (\mathrm{t} _ {\mathrm{i}}) - \mathrm{W} (\mathrm{t} _ {\mathrm{i} - 1}) \} \right] \\ & = \sum_ {\mathrm{i} = 1} ^ {\mathrm{n}} \left[ \min \left(\tau_ {\mathrm{i}}, \mathrm{t} _ {\mathrm{i}}\right) - \min \left(\tau_ {\mathrm{i}}, \mathrm{t} _ {\mathrm{i} - 1}\right) \right] = \sum_ {\mathrm{i} = 1} ^ {\mathrm{n}} \left(\tau_ {\mathrm{i}} - \mathrm{t} _ {\mathrm{i} - 1}\right) \end{array}\tag{4.4.7}
$$

Let us set for all i 

$$
\tau_ {\mathrm{i}} = \alpha t _ {\mathrm{i}} + (1 - \alpha) t _ {\mathrm{i} - 1} 0 \leq \alpha \leq 1\tag{4.4.8}
$$

Substitution of (4.4.8) into (4.4.7) leads to $\mathrm { { E } } [ \mathrm { { S } } _ { \mathrm { { n } } } ] = \alpha \mathrm { { T } }$ . Hence, the sum (4.4.7) depends on the arbitrary parameter a and therefore can have any value. Within the Ito’s formalism, the value $\mathsf Q = 0$ is chosen, so that $\tau _ { \mathrm { i } } = \mathrm { t _ { i - l } }$ . The stochastic Ito’s integral is defined as 

$$
\int_ {0} ^ {T} f (t) d W (t) = m s - \lim _ {n \rightarrow \infty} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} f \left(t _ {i - 1}\right)\left[ W \left(t _ {i}\right) - W \left(t _ {i - 1}\right)\right]\tag{4.4.9}
$$

The notation ms-lim stands for the mean-square limit. It means that the difference between the Ito integral in the left-hand side of (4.4.9) and the sum in the right-hand side of (4.4.9) has variance that approaches zero as n increases to infinity. Thus, (4.4.9) is equivalent to 

$$
\lim _ {n \rightarrow \infty} E \left[ \int_ {0} ^ {T} f (t) d W (t) - \sum_ {i - 1} ^ {n} f \left(t _ {i - 1}\right)\left\{W \left(t _ {i}\right) - W \left(t _ {i - 1}\right)\right\}\right] ^ {2} = 0\tag{4.4.10}
$$

Let us consider the integral 

$$
\mathrm{I} \left(t _ {2}, t _ {1}\right) = \int_ {t _ {1}} ^ {t _ {2}} \mathrm{W} (t) \mathrm{dW} (t)\tag{4.4.11}
$$

as an example of calculating the Ito’s integral. If the function W(t) is deterministic, then the Riemann-Stieltjes integral $\mathbf { I } _ { \mathrm { R - S } } ( \mathrm { t } _ { 2 } , \mathrm { t } _ { 1 } )$ equals 

$$
\mathrm{I} _ {\mathrm{R} - \mathrm{S}} (\mathrm{t} _ {2}, \mathrm{t} _ {1}) = 0. 5 [ \mathrm{W} (\mathrm{t} _ {2}) ^ {2} - \mathrm{W} (\mathrm{t} _ {1}) ^ {2} ]\tag{4.4.12}
$$

However, when W(t) is the Wiener process, the Ito’s integral $\mathrm { I } _ { \mathrm { I } } ( \mathrm { t } _ { 2 } , \mathrm { t } _ { 1 } )$ leads to a somewhat unexpected result 

$$
\mathrm{I} _ {\mathrm{I}} \left(\mathrm{t} _ {2}, \mathrm{t} _ {1}\right) = 0. 5 \left[ \mathrm{W} \left(\mathrm{t} _ {2}\right) ^ {2} - \mathrm{W} \left(\mathrm{t} _ {1}\right) ^ {2} - \left(\mathrm{t} _ {2} - \mathrm{t} _ {1}\right) \right]\tag{4.4.13}
$$

This follows directly from equation (4.3.6). Obviously, the result (4.4.13) can be derived directly from the definition of the Ito’s integral (see Exercise 1). Note that the mean of the Ito’s integral (4.4.11) equals zero 

$$
\mathrm{E} [ \mathrm{I} _ {\mathrm{I}} (t _ {2}, t _ {1}) ] = 0\tag{4.4.14}
$$

The difference between the right-hand sides of (4.4.12) and (4.4.13) is determined by the particular choice of ${ \mathfrak { X } } = 0$ in (4.4.8). Stratonovich has offered another definition of the stochastic integral by choosing $\alpha = 0 . 5$ . In contrast to equation (4.4.9), the Stratonovich’s integral is defined as 

$$
\int_ {0} ^ {T} f (t) d W (t) = m s - \lim _ {n \rightarrow \infty} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} f \left(\frac {t _ {i - 1} + t _ {i}}{2}\right) [ W (t _ {i}) - W (t _ {i - 1}) ]\tag{4.4.15}
$$

For the integrand in (4.4.11), the Stratonovich’s integral $\mathrm { I } _ { \mathrm { S } } ( \mathrm { t } _ { 2 } , \mathrm { t } _ { 1 } )$ coincides with the Riemann-Stieltjes integral 

$$
\mathrm{I} _ {\mathrm{S}} \left(\mathrm{t} _ {2}, \mathrm{t} _ {1}\right) = 0. 5 \left[ \mathrm{W} \left(\mathrm{t} _ {2}\right) ^ {2} - \mathrm{W} \left(\mathrm{t} _ {1}\right) ^ {2} \right]\tag{4.4.16}
$$

Both Ito’s and Stratonovich’s formulations can be transformed into each other. In particular, the Ito’s stochastic differential equation (4.3.1) 

$$
\mathrm{dy} _ {\mathrm{I}} (t) = \mu \mathrm{dt} + \sigma \mathrm{dW} (t)\tag{4.4.17}
$$

is equivalent to the Stratonovich’s equation 

$$
\mathrm{dy} _ {\mathrm{S}} (t) = \left[ \mu - 0. 5 \sigma \frac {\partial \sigma}{\partial y} \right] \mathrm{dt} + \sigma \mathrm{dW} (t)\tag{4.4.18}
$$

The applications of stochastic calculus in finance are based almost exclusively on the Ito’s theory. Consider, for example, the integral 

$$
\int_ {t _ {1}} ^ {t _ {2}} \sigma (t) d W (t)\tag{4.4.19}
$$

If no correlation between the function $\sigma ( \mathrm { t } )$ and the innovation dW(t) is assumed, then the Ito’s approximation is a natural choice. In this case, the function s(t) is said to be a nonanticipating function [1, 2]. However, if the innovations dW(t) are correlated (so-called non-white noise), then the Stratonovich’s approximation appears to be an adequate theory [1, 6]. 

## 4.5 MARTINGALES

The martingale methodology plays an important role in the modern theory of finance [2, 7, 8]. Martingale is a stochastic process X(t) that satisfies the following condition 

$$
\operatorname{E} [ \mathrm{X} (t + 1) | \mathrm{X} (t), \mathrm{X} (t - 1), \dots ] = \mathrm{X} (t)\tag{4.5.1}
$$

The equivalent definition is given by 

$$
\operatorname{E} [ \mathrm{X} (t + 1) - \mathrm{X} (t) | \mathrm{X} (t), \mathrm{X} (t - 1), \dots ] = 0\tag{4.5.2}
$$

Both these definitions are easily generalized for the continuum presentation where the time interval, dt, between two sequent moments t þ 1 and t approaches zero (dt ! 0). The notion of martingale is rooted in the gambling theory. It is closely associated with the notion of fair game, in which none of the players has an advantage. The condition (4.5.1) implies that the expectation of the gamer wealth at time t þ 1 conditioned on the entire history of the game is equal to the gamer wealth at time t. Similarly, equation (4.5.2) means that the expectation to win at every round of the game being conditioned on the history of the game equals zero. In other words, martingale has no trend. A process that has positive trend is named submartingale. A process with negative trend is called supermartingale. 

The martingale hypothesis applied to the asset prices states that the expectation of future price is simply the current price. This assumption is closely related to the Efficient Market Hypothesis discussed in Section 2.3. Generally, the asset prices are not martingales for they incorporate risk premium. Indeed, there must be some reward offered to investors for bearing the risks associated with keeping the assets. It can be shown, however, that the prices with discounted risk premium are martingales [3]. 

The important property of the Ito’s integral is that it is martingale. Consider, for example, the integral (4.4.19) approximated with the sum (4.4.9). Because the innovations dW(t) are unpredictable, it follows from (4.4.14) that 

$$
\mathrm{E} \left[ \int_ {t} ^ {t + \Delta t} \sigma (z) \mathrm{dW} (z) \right] = 0\tag{4.5.3}
$$

Therefore, 

$$
\mathrm{E} \left[ \int_ {0} ^ {\mathrm{t} + \Delta \mathrm{t}} \sigma (\mathrm{z}) \mathrm{dW} (\mathrm{z}) \right] = \int_ {0} ^ {\mathrm{t}} \sigma (\mathrm{z}) \mathrm{dW} (\mathrm{z})\tag{4.5.4}
$$

and the integral (4.4.19) satisfies the martingale definition. Note that for the Brownian motion with drift (4.2.14) 

$$
\mathrm{E} [ \mathrm{y} (\mathrm{t} + \mathrm{dt}) ] = \mathrm{E} \left[ \mathrm{y} (\mathrm{t}) + \int_ {\mathrm{t}} ^ {\mathrm{t} + \mathrm{dt}} \mathrm{dy} \right] = \mathrm{y} (\mathrm{t}) + \mu \mathrm{dt}\tag{4.5.5}
$$

Hence, the Brownian motion with drift is not a martingale. However, the process 

$$
\mathrm{z(t)=y(t)-} \mu \mathrm{t}\tag{4.5.6}
$$

is a martingale since 

$$
\operatorname{E} [ \mathrm{z(t+dt)} ] = \mathrm{z(t)}\tag{4.5.7}
$$

This result follows also from the Doob-Meyer decomposition theorem, which states that a continuous submartingale X(t) at $0 \leq \mathfrak { t } \leq \infty$ with finite expectation $\mathrm { E } [ \mathrm { X ( t ) } ] < \infty$ can be decomposed into a continuous martingale and an increasing deterministic process. 

## 4.6 REFERENCES FOR FURTHER READING

Theory and applications of the stochastic processes in natural sciences are described in [1, 6]. A good introduction to the stochastic calculus in finance is given in [2]. For a mathematically inclined reader, the presentation of the stochastic theory with increasing level of technical details can be found in [7, 8]. 

## 4.7 EXERCISES

1. Simulate daily price returns using the geometric Brownian motion (4.3.7) for four years. Use equation (4.2.15) for approximating DW. Assume that $\mathrm { S } ( 0 ) = 1 0 , \mu = 1 0 \% , \sigma = 2 0 \%$ (m and s are given per annum). Assume 250 working days per annum. 

2. Prove that 

$$
\int_ {t _ {1}} ^ {t _ {2}} \mathrm{W} (s) ^ {n} d \mathrm{W} (s) = \frac {1}{n + 1} \left[ \mathrm{W} \left(t _ {2}\right) ^ {n + 1} - \mathrm{W} \left(t _ {1}\right) ^ {n + 1} \right] - \frac {n}{2} \int_ {t _ {1}} ^ {t _ {2}} \mathrm{W} (s) ^ {n - 1} d s
$$

Hint: Calculate $\mathrm { d } ( \mathbb { W } ^ { + 1 } )$ using the Ito’s lemma. 

3. Solve the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck equation that describes the meanreverting process in which the solution fluctuates around its mean 

$$
\mathrm{d} \mathbf {X} = - \mu \mathbf {X} \mathrm{d} t + \sigma \mathrm{d} \mathbf {W}, \mu > 0
$$

Hint: introduce the variable $\mathbf { Y } = \mathbf { X } \exp \left( \mu \mathrm { t } \right)$ 

*4. Derive the integral (4.4.13) directly from the definition of the Ito’s integral (4.4.9). 

## Time Series Analysis

Time series analysis has become an indispensable theoretical tool in financial and economic research. Section 5.1 is devoted to the commonly used univariate autoregressive and moving average models. The means for modeling trends and seasonality effects are described in Section 5.2. The processes with non-stationary variance (conditional heteroskedasticity) are discussed in Section 5.3. Finally, the specifics of the multivariate time series are introduced in Section 5.4. 

## 5.1 AUTOREGRESSIVE AND MOVING AVERAGE MODELS

## 5.1.1 AUTOREGRESSIVE MODEL

First, we shall consider a univariate time series y(t) for a process that is observed at moments $\mathsf { t } = 0 , 1 , . . . , \mathsf { n } ( \mathrm { s e e } , \mathsf { e . g . } , [ 1 , 2 ] )$ . The time series in which the observation at moment t depends linearly on several lagged observations at moments $\mathfrak { t } - 1 , \mathfrak { t } - 2 , \dotsc , \mathfrak { t } - \mathfrak { p }$ 

$$
\mathrm{y} (t) = a _ {1} \mathrm{y} (t - 1) + a _ {2} \mathrm{y} (t - 2) + \dots + a _ {\mathrm{p}} \mathrm{y} (t - p) + \varepsilon (t), t > p\tag{5.1.1}
$$

is called the autoregressiveprocess oforder p, or AR(p). The term e(t) in (5.1.1) is the white noise that satisfies the conditions (4.2.6). The lag operator $\mathbf { L } ^ { \mathsf { p } } = \mathbf { y } ( \mathbf { t } - \mathsf { p } )$ is often used for describing time series. Note that $\bar { \mathbf { L } ^ { 0 } } = \mathbf { y ( t ) }$ . Equation (5.1.1) in terms of the lag operator has the form 

$$
\mathrm {A_ {p} (L)y(t) = \varepsilon(t)}\tag{5.1.2}
$$

where 

$$
\mathrm {A_ {p} (L) = 1- a_ {1} L- a_ {2} L^ {2} -\ldots- a_ {p} L^ {p}}\tag{5.1.3}
$$

The operator $\mathrm { A } _ { \mathrm { p } } ( \mathrm { L } )$ is called the AR polynomial in lag operator of order p. Let us consider AR(1) that starts with a random shock. Its definition implies that 

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathrm {y(0) = \varepsilon(0), y(1) = a_ {1} y(0)+ \varepsilon(1),} \\ \mathrm {y(2) = a_ {1} y(1) + \varepsilon(2) = a_ {1} ^ {2} \varepsilon(0)+ a_ {1} \varepsilon(1) + \varepsilon(2), \ldots} \end{array}
$$

Hence, by induction, 

$$
\mathrm{y} (t) = \sum_ {\mathrm{i} = 0} ^ {t} a _ {1} ^ {\mathrm{i}} \varepsilon (t - \mathrm{i})\tag{5.1.4}
$$

Mean and variance of AR(1) equal, respectively 

$$
\operatorname{E} [ \mathrm{y(t)} ] = 0, \operatorname{Var} [ \mathrm{y(t)} ] = \sigma^ {2} / (1 - a _ {1} ^ {2}),\tag{5.1.5}
$$

Obviously, the contributions of the ‘‘old’’ noise converge with time to zero when $\left| \mathrm { a } _ { 1 } \right| < 1$ . As a result, this process does not drift too far from its mean. This feature is named mean reversion. 

The process with ${ \bf a } _ { 1 } = 1$ is called the random walk 

$$
\mathrm{y} (t) = \mathrm{y} (t - 1) + \varepsilon (t)\tag{5.1.6}
$$

In this case, equation (5.1.4) reduces to 

$$
\mathrm{y(t)} = \sum_ {\mathrm{i=0}} ^ {\mathrm{t}} \varepsilon (\mathrm{t-i})
$$

The noise contributions to the random walk do not weaken with time. Therefore, the random walk does not exhibit mean reversion. Now, consider the process that represents the first difference 

$$
\mathrm{x} (t) = \mathrm{y} (t) - \mathrm{y} (t - 1) = \varepsilon (t)\tag{5.1.7}
$$

Obviously, past noise has only transitory character for the process $\mathbf { \boldsymbol { x } } ( \mathbf { \boldsymbol { t } } )$ . Therefore, x(t) is mean-reverting. Some processes must be differenced several times in order to exclude non-transitory noise shocks. The processes differenced d times are named integrated of order d and denoted as I(d). The differencing operator is used for describing an I(d) process 

$$
\Delta_ {i} ^ {d} = (1 - L ^ {i}) ^ {d}, j, d = \dots , - 2, - 1, 0, 1, 2 \dots\tag{5.1.8}
$$

If an I(d) process can be reduced to $\operatorname { A R } ( { \mathfrak { p } } )$ process while applying the differencing operator, it is named $\mathrm { A R I } ( { \mathfrak { p } } , { \mathfrak { d } } )$ process and has the form: 

$$
\Delta_ {1} ^ {\mathrm{d}} \mathrm{y} (t) - a _ {1} \Delta_ {1} ^ {\mathrm{d}} \mathrm{y} (t - 1) - \dots - a _ {\mathrm{p}} \Delta_ {1} ^ {\mathrm{d}} \mathrm{y} (t - \mathrm{p}) = \varepsilon (t), t \geq \mathrm{p} + \mathrm{d}\tag{5.1.9}
$$

Note that differencing a time series d times reduces the number of independent variables by d, so that the total number of independent variables in ARI(p, d) within the sample with n observations equals $\boldsymbol { \mathrm { n } } - \boldsymbol { \mathrm { p } } - \boldsymbol { \mathrm { d } }$ 

The unit root is another notion widely used for discerning permanent and transitory effects of random shocks. It is based on the roots of the characteristic polynomial for the $\operatorname { A R } ( { \mathfrak { p } } )$ model. For example, $\mathbf { A R } ( 1 )$ has the characteristic polynomial 

$$
1 - \mathrm{a} _ {1} \mathrm{z} = 0\tag{5.1.10}
$$

If ${ \mathrm { a } } _ { 1 } = 1$ , then $z = 1$ and the characteristic polynomial has the unit root. In general, the characteristic polynomial roots can have complex values. The solution to equation (5.1.10) is outside the unit circle $( \mathrm { i } . \mathrm { e } . , z > 1 )$ when $\mathrm { a } _ { 1 } < 1$ . It can be shown that all solutions for $\operatorname { A R } ( { \mathfrak { p } } )$ are outside the unit circle when 

$$
1 - \mathrm {a_ {1} z- a_ {2} z^ {2} -\ldots- a_ {p} z^ {p} = 0}\tag{5.1.11}
$$

## 5.1.2 MOVING AVERAGE MODELS

A model more general than $\operatorname { A R } ( { \mathfrak { p } } )$ contains both lagged observations and lagged noise 

$$
\begin{array}{c} \mathrm {y(t) = a_ {1} y(t - 1)+ a_ {2} y(t - 2) + \ldots+ a_ {p} y(t - p) + \varepsilon(t)} \\ + b _ {1} \varepsilon (t - 1) + b _ {2} \varepsilon (t - 2) + \ldots + b _ {q} \varepsilon (t - q) \end{array}\tag{5.1.12}
$$

This model is called autoregressive moving average model of order $( \mathfrak { p } , \mathfrak { q } )$ , or simply $\mathbf { A R M A ( p , q ) }$ . Sometimes modeling of empirical data requires AR(p) with a rather high number p. Then, ARMA(p, q) may be more efficient in that the total number of its terms $( \mathfrak { p } + \mathfrak { q } )$ needed for given accuracy is lower than the number p in AR(p). ARMA(p, q) can be expanded into the integrated model, ARIMA(p, d, q), similar to the expansion of $\operatorname { A R } ( { \mathfrak { p } } )$ into ARI(p, d). Neglecting the autoregressive terms in $\mathbf { A R M A ( p , \ p ) }$ yields ${ \mathrm { ~ a ~ } } ^ { \ast } { \mathrm { p u r e } } ^ { \prime \prime }$ moving average model MA(q) 

$$
\mathrm{y} (t) = \varepsilon (t) + \mathrm{b} _ {1} \varepsilon (t - 1) + \mathrm{b} _ {2} \varepsilon (t - 2) + \dots + \mathrm{b} _ {\mathrm{q}} \varepsilon (t - \mathrm{q})\tag{5.1.13}
$$

MA(q) can be presented in the form 

$$
\mathrm{y} (t) = \mathrm{B} _ {\mathrm{q}} (\mathrm{L}) \varepsilon (t)\tag{5.1.14}
$$

where $\mathrm { \mathbf { B } _ { q } ( L ) }$ is the MA polynomial in lag operator 

$$
\mathrm{B} _ {\mathrm{q}} (\mathrm{L}) = 1 + \mathrm{b} _ {1} \mathrm{L} + \mathrm{b} _ {2} \mathrm{L} ^ {2} + \dots + \mathrm{b} _ {\mathrm{q}} \mathrm{L} ^ {\mathrm{q}}\tag{5.1.15}
$$

The moving average model does not depend explicitly on the lagged values of y(t). Yet, it is easy to show that this model implicitly incorporates the past. Consider, for example, the MA(1) model 

$$
\mathrm{y} (t) = \varepsilon (t) + b _ {1} \varepsilon (t - 1)\tag{5.1.16}
$$

with $\varepsilon ( 0 ) = 0$ . For this model, 

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathrm {y(1) = \varepsilon(1), y(2) = \varepsilon(2)+ b_ {1} \varepsilon(1) = \varepsilon(2)+ b_ {1} y(1)}, \\ \mathrm {y(3) = \varepsilon(3)+ b_ {1} \varepsilon(2) = \varepsilon(3)+ b_ {1} (y(2)- b_ {1} y(1)),\ldots} \end{array}
$$

Thus, the general result for MA(1) has the form 

$$
\mathrm{y} (t) \left(1 - \mathrm{b} _ {1} \mathrm{L} + \mathrm{b} _ {1} \mathrm{L} ^ {2} - \mathrm{b} _ {1} \mathrm{L} ^ {3} + \dots\right) = \varepsilon (t)\tag{5.1.17}
$$

Equation (5.1.17) can be viewed as the AR(1) process, which illustrates that the MA model does depend on past. 

The MA(q) model is invertible if it can be transformed into an $\mathbf { A R } ( \infty )$ model. It can be shown that MA(q) is invertible if all solutions to the equation 

$$
1 + \mathrm{b} _ {1} \mathrm{z} + \mathrm{b} _ {2} \mathrm{z} ^ {2} + \dots + \mathrm{b} _ {\mathrm{q}} \mathrm{z} ^ {\mathrm{q}} = 0\tag{5.1.18}
$$

are outside the unit circle. In particular, MA(1) is invertible if $| \mathsf { b } _ { 1 } | < 1$ . If the process y(t) has a non-zero mean value m, then the AR(1) model can be presented in the following form 

$$
\mathrm{y} (t) - \mathrm{m} = a _ {1} [ \mathrm{y} (t - 1) - \mathrm{m} ] + \varepsilon (t) = c + a _ {1} \mathrm{y} (t - 1) + \varepsilon (t)\tag{5.1.19}
$$

In (5.1.19), intercept c equals: 

$$
\mathrm{c} = \mathrm{m} (1 - a _ {1})\tag{5.1.20}
$$

The general AR(p) model with a non-zero mean has the following form 

$$
\mathrm{A} _ {\mathrm{p}} (\mathrm{L}) \mathrm{y} (\mathrm{t}) = \mathrm{c} + \varepsilon (\mathrm{t}), \mathrm{c} = \mathrm{m} (1 - \mathrm{a} _ {1} - \dots \mathrm{a} _ {\mathrm{p}})\tag{5.1.21}
$$

Similarly, the intercept can be included into the general moving average model MA(q) 

$$
\mathrm{y} (t) = \mathrm{c} + \mathrm{B} _ {\mathrm{p}} (\mathrm{L}) \varepsilon (t), \mathrm{c} = \mathrm{m}\tag{5.1.22}
$$

Note that mean of the MA model coincides with its intercept because mean of the white noise is zero. 

## 5.1.3 AUTOCORRELATION AND FORECASTING

Now, let us introduce the autocorrelation function (ACF) for process y(t) 

$$
\rho (\mathrm{k}) = \gamma (\mathrm{k}) / \gamma (0)\tag{5.1.23}
$$

where g(k) is the autocovariance of order k 

$$
\gamma (\mathrm{k}) = \operatorname{E} [ \mathrm{y(t)} - \mathrm{m}) (\mathrm{y(t-k)} - \mathrm{m}) ]\tag{5.1.24}
$$

The autocorrelation functions may have some typical patterns, which can be used for identification of empirical time series [2]. The obvious properties of ACF are 

$$
\rho (0) = 1, - 1 <   \rho (\mathrm{k}) <   1 \text { for } \mathrm{k} \neq 0\tag{5.1.25}
$$

ACF is closely related to the ARMA parameters. In particular, for AR(1) 

$$
\rho (1) = a _ {1}\tag{5.1.26}
$$

The ACF of the first order for MA(1) equals 

$$
\rho (1) = \mathrm{b} _ {1} / (\mathrm{b} _ {1} ^ {2} + 1)\tag{5.1.27}
$$

The right-hand side of the expression (5.1.27) has the same value for the inverse transform $\mathsf { b } _ { 1 } \to 1 / \mathsf { b } _ { 1 }$ . For example, two processes 

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathrm{x(t)} = \varepsilon (\mathrm{t}) + 2 \varepsilon (\mathrm{t-1}) \\ \mathrm{y(t)} = \varepsilon (\mathrm{t}) + 0. 5 \varepsilon (\mathrm{t-1}) \end{array}
$$

have the same $\rho ( 1 )$ . Note, however, that y(t) is an invertible process while x(t) is not. 

ARMA modeling is widely used for forecasting. Consider a forecast of a variable $\mathrm { y } ( \mathrm { t } + 1 )$ based on a set of n variables x(t) known at moment t. This set can be just past values of y, that is, $\operatorname { y ( t ) } , \operatorname { y ( t - 1 ) } , \ldots , \operatorname { y ( t - n + 1 ) }$ . Let us denote the forecast with ${ \hat { \mathbf { y } } } ( t + 1 | t )$ . The quality of forecast is usually defined with the some loss function. The mean squared error (MSE) is the conventional loss function in many applications 

$$
\mathrm{MSE} (\hat {\mathbf {y}} (t + 1 | t)) = \mathrm{E} [ (\mathbf {y} (t + 1) - \hat {\mathbf {y}} (t + 1 | t)) ^ {2} ]\tag{5.1.28}
$$

The forecast that yields the minimum of MSE turns out to be the expectation of $\mathrm { y } ( \mathrm { t } + 1 )$ conditioned on x(t) 

$$
\hat {\mathbf {y}} (t + 1 | t) = \operatorname{E} [ \mathbf {y} (t + 1) | \mathbf {x} (t) ]\tag{5.1.29}
$$

In the case of linear regression 

$$
\mathbf {y} (t + 1) = \mathbf {b} ^ {\prime} \mathbf {x} (t) + \varepsilon (t)\tag{5.1.30}
$$

MSE is reduced to the ordinary least squares (OLS) estimate for b. For a sample with T observations, 

$$
\mathbf {b} = \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {\mathrm{T}} \mathbf {x} (t) \mathbf {y} (t + 1) / \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {\mathrm{T}} \mathbf {x} (t) \mathbf {x} ^ {\prime} (t)\tag{5.1.31}
$$

Another important concept in the time series analysis is the maximum likelihood estimate (MLE) [2]. Consider the general ARMA model (5.1.12) with the white noise (4.2.6). The problem is how to estimate the ARMA parameters on the basis of given observations of y(t). The idea of MLE is to find such a vector ${ \bf r } ^ { \prime } = ( { \mathrm { a } } _ { 1 } , \dots , { \mathrm { a } } _ { { \mathrm { p } } } , \dots ,$ $\mathsf { b } _ { 1 } , \hdots , \mathsf { b } _ { \mathrm { q } } , \sigma ^ { 2 } )$ that maximizes the likelihood function for given observations $( \mathbf { y } _ { 1 } , \mathbf { y } _ { 2 } , \ldots , \mathbf { y } _ { \mathrm { T } } )$ 

$$
\mathrm{f} _ {1, 2}, \dots , \mathrm{T} (y _ {1}, y _ {2}, \dots , y _ {T}; \mathbf {r} ^ {\prime})\tag{5.1.32}
$$

The likelihood function (5.1.32) has the sense of probability of observing the data sample $( \mathbf { y } _ { 1 } , \mathbf { y } _ { 2 } , \ldots , \mathbf { y } _ { \mathrm { T } } )$ . In this approach, the ARMA model and the probability distribution for the white noise should be specified first. Often the normal distribution leads to reasonable estimates even if the real distribution is different. Furthermore, the likelihood function must be calculated for the chosen ARMA model. Finally, the components of the vector $\mathbf { r } ^ { \prime }$ must be estimated. The latter step may require sophisticated numerical optimization technique. Details of implementation of MLE are discussed in [2]. 

## 5.2 TRENDS AND SEASONALITY

Finding trends is an important part of the time series analysis. Presence of trend implies that the time series has no mean reversion. Moreover, mean and variance of a trending process depend on the sample. The time series with trend is named non-stationary. If a process y(t) is stationary, its mean, variance, and autocovariance are finite and do not depend on time. This implies that autocovariance (5.1.24) depends only on the lag parameter k. Such a definition of stationarity is also called covariance-stationarity or weak stationarity because it does not impose any restrictions on the higher moments of the process. Strict stationarity implies that higher moments also do not depend on time. Note that any MA process is covariance-station ary. However, the AR(p) process is covariance-stationary only if the roots of its polynomial are outside the unit circle. 

It is important to discern deterministic trend and stochastic trend. They have a different nature yet their graphs may look sometimes very similar [1]. Consider first the AR(1) model with the deterministic trend 

$$
\mathrm{y} (\mathrm{t}) - \mathrm{m} - \mathrm{ct} = \mathrm{a} _ {1} (\mathrm{y} (\mathrm{t} - 1) - \mathrm{m} - \mathrm{c} (\mathrm{t} - 1)) + \varepsilon (\mathrm{t})\tag{5.2.1}
$$

Let us introduce $\mathtt { z ( t ) } = \mathtt { y ( t ) } - \mathtt { m } - \mathtt { c t }$ . Then equation (5.2.1) has the solution 

$$
z (t) = a _ {1} ^ {t} z (0) + \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {t} a _ {1} ^ {t - i} \varepsilon (t)\tag{5.2.2}
$$

where $\boldsymbol z ( 0 )$ is a pre-sample starting value of z. Obviously, the random shocks are transitory $\mathrm { i f \left| a _ { l } \right| < 1 }$ . The trend incorporated in the definition of z(t) is deterministic when $\left| \mathrm { a } _ { 1 } \right| < 1$ . However, if ${ \bf a } _ { 1 } = 1$ , then equation (5.2.1) has the form 

$$
\mathrm{y} (t) = \mathrm{c} + \mathrm{y} (t - 1) + \varepsilon (t)\tag{5.2.3}
$$

The process (5.2.3) is named the random walk with drift. In this case, equation (5.2.2) is reduced to 

$$
\mathrm{z(t)} = \mathrm{z(0)} + \sum_ {\mathrm{i} = 1} ^ {\mathrm{t}} \varepsilon (\mathrm{t})\tag{5.2.4}
$$

The sum of non-transitory shocks in the right-hand side of equation (5.2.4) is named stochastic trend. Consider, for example, the deterministic trend model with m ¼ 0 and e(t) ¼ N(0, 1) 

$$
\mathrm{y} (t) = 0. 1 t + \varepsilon (t)\tag{5.2.5}
$$

and the stochastic trend model 

$$
\mathrm{y} (t) = 0. 1 + \mathrm{y} (t - 1) + \varepsilon (t), \mathrm{y} (0) = 0\tag{5.2.6}
$$

As it can be seen from Figure 5.1, both graphs look similar. In general, however, the stochastic trend model can deviate from the deterministic trend for a long time. 

Stochastic trend implies that the process is I(1). Then the lag polynomial (5.1.3) can be represented in the form 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-11-17/6bce6541-8c3d-4db5-b1d8-4bdc9ce1043d/218a56e95681a59142460bcbbf582475b5070a081bbf282effcde96180e3207c.jpg)



Figure 5.1 Deterministic and stochastic trends: A - equation (5.2.5), B - equation (5.2.6).


$$
\mathrm {A_ {p} (L) = (1- L) A _ {p - 1} (L)}\tag{5.2.7}
$$

Similarly, the process I(2) has the lag polynomial 

$$
\mathrm {A_ {p} (L) = (1- L) ^ {2} A_ {p - 2} (L)}\tag{5.2.8}
$$

and so on. The standard procedure for testing presence of the unit root in time series is the Dickey-Fuller method [1, 2]. This method is implemented in major econometric software packages (see the Section 5.5). 

Seasonal effects may play an important role in the properties of time series. Sometimes, there is a need to eliminate these effects in order to focus on the stochastic specifics of the process. Various differencing filters can be used for achieving this goal [2]. In other cases, seasonal effect itself may be the object of interest. The general approach for handling seasonal effects is introducing dummy parameters D(s, t) where $\mathbf { s } = 1 , 2 , \ldots , \mathbf { S } ; \mathbf { S }$ is the number of seasons. For example, ${ \bf S } = 1 2$ is used for modeling the monthly effects. Then the parameter D(s, t) equals 1 at a specific season s and equals zero at all other seasons. The seasonal extension of an $\mathbf { A R M A ( p , q ) }$ model has the following form 

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathrm {y(t) = a_ {1} y(t - 1)+ a_ {2} y(t - 2) + \ldots+ a_ {p} y(t - p) + \varepsilon(t)} \\ + b _ {1} \varepsilon (t - 1) + b _ {2} \varepsilon (t - 2) + \ldots + b _ {q} \varepsilon (t - q) + \sum_ {s = 1} ^ {S} d _ {s} D (s, t) \end{array}\tag{5.2.9}
$$

Note that forecasting with the model (5.2.9) requires estimating $\left( \mathbf { p } + \mathbf { q } + \mathbf { S } \right)$ parameters. 

## 5.3 CONDITIONAL HETEROSKEDASTICITY

So far, we considered random processes with the white noise (4.2.6) that are characterized with constant unconditional variance. Condi tional variance has not been discussed so far. In general, the processes with unspecified conditional variance are named homoskedastic. Many random time series are not well described with the IID process. In particular, there may be strong positive autocorrelation in squared asset returns. This means that large returns (either positive or negative) follow large returns. In this case, it is said that the return volatility is clustered. The effect of volatility clustering is also called autoregressive conditional heteroskedasticity (ARCH). It should be noted that small autocorrelation in squared returns does not necessarily mean that there is no volatility clustering. Strong outliers that lead to high values of skewness and kurtosis may lower autocorrela tion. If these outliers are removed from the sample, volatility clustering may become apparent [3]. 

Several models in which past shocks contribute to the current volatility have been developed. Generally, they are rooted in the ARCH(m) model where the conditional variance is a weighed sum of m squared lagged returns 

$$
\sigma^ {2} (t) = \omega + a _ {1} \varepsilon^ {2} (t - 1) + a _ {2} \varepsilon^ {2} (t - 2) + \dots + a _ {m} \varepsilon^ {2} (t - m)\tag{5.3.1}
$$

In $( 5 . 3 . 1 ) , \ \varepsilon ( \mathrm { t } ) \sim \mathrm { N } ( 0 , \sigma ^ { 2 } ( \mathrm { t } ) ) , \omega > 0 , \mathrm { a } _ { 1 } , . . . , \mathrm { a } _ { \mathrm { m } } \geq 0 .$ Unfortunately, application of the ARCH(m) process to modeling the financial time series often requires polynomials with high order m. A more efficient model is the generalized ARCH (GARCH) process. The GARCH (m, n) process combines the ARCH(m) process with the AR(n) process for lagged variance 

$$
\begin{array}{c} \sigma^ {2} (t) = \omega + a _ {1} \varepsilon^ {2} (t - 1) + a _ {2} \varepsilon^ {2} (t - 2) + \ldots + a _ {m} \varepsilon^ {2} (t - m) \\ + b _ {1} \sigma^ {2} (t - 1) + b _ {2} \sigma^ {2} (t - 2) + \ldots + b _ {n} \sigma^ {2} (t - n) \end{array}\tag{5.3.2}
$$

The simple GARCH(1, 1) model is widely used in applications 

$$
\sigma^ {2} (t) = \omega + a \varepsilon^ {2} (t - 1) + b \sigma^ {2} (t - 1)\tag{5.3.3}
$$

Equation (5.3.3) can be transformed into 

$$
\sigma^ {2} (t) = \omega + (a + b) \sigma^ {2} (t - 1) + a [ \varepsilon^ {2} (t) - \sigma^ {2} (t - 1) ]\tag{5.3.4}
$$

The last term in equation (5.3.4) is conditioned on information avail able at time (t  1) and has zero mean. This term can be treated as a shock to volatility. Therefore, the unconditional expectation of volatility for the GARCH(1, 1) model equals 

$$
\operatorname{E} [ \sigma^ {2} (t) ] = \omega / (1 - a - b)\tag{5.3.5}
$$

This implies that the GARCH(1, 1) process is weakly stationary when $\ a + \ b < 1$ . The advantage of the stationary GARCH(1, 1) model is that it can be easily used for forecasting. Namely, the conditional expectation of volatility at time (t þ k) equals [4] 

$$
\mathrm{E} \left[ \sigma^ {2} (\mathrm{t} + \mathrm{k}) \right] = (\mathrm{a} + \mathrm{b}) ^ {\mathrm{k}} \left[ \sigma^ {2} (\mathrm{t}) - \omega / (1 - \mathrm{a} - \mathrm{b}) \right] + \omega / (1 - \mathrm{a} - \mathrm{b})\tag{5.3.6}
$$

The GARCH(1, 1) model (5.3.4) can be rewritten as 

$$
\sigma^ {2} (t) = \omega / (1 - b) + a (\varepsilon^ {2} (t - 1) + b \varepsilon^ {2} (t - 2) + b ^ {2} \varepsilon^ {2} (t - 3) + \dots)\tag{5.3.7}
$$

Equation (5.3.7) shows that the GARCH(1, 1) model is equivalent to the infinite ARCH model with exponentially weighed coefficients. This explains why the GARCH models are more efficient than the ARCH models. 

Several GARCH models have been described in the econometric literature [1–3]. One popular GARCH(1, 1) model with $\mathbf { a } + \mathbf { b } = 1$ is called integrated GARCH (IGARCH). It has the autoregressive unit root. Therefore volatility of this process follows random walk and can be easily forecasted 

$$
\operatorname{E} [ \sigma^ {2} (t + k) ] = \sigma^ {2} (t) + k \omega\tag{5.3.8}
$$

IGARCH can be presented in the form 

$$
\sigma^ {2} (t) = \omega + (1 - \lambda) \varepsilon^ {2} (t - 1) + \lambda \sigma^ {2} (t - 1)\tag{5.3.9}
$$

where $0 < \lambda < 1$ . If v ¼ 0, IGARCH coincides with the exponentially weighed moving average (EWMA) 

$$
\sigma^ {2} (t) = (1 - \lambda) \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \lambda^ {i - 1} \varepsilon^ {2} (t - i)\tag{5.3.10}
$$

Indeed, the n-period EWMA for a time series y(t) is defined as 

$$
\begin{array}{c} \mathrm {z(t) = [y(t - 1)+ \lambda y(t - 2)+ \lambda^ {2} y(t - 3)+ \dots +} \\ \lambda^ {\mathrm{n-1}} \mathrm {y(t - n)] / (1+ \lambda + \dots + \lambda^ {\mathrm{n}})} \end{array}\tag{5.3.11}
$$

where $0 < \lambda < 1$ . For large n, the denominator of (5.3.11) converges to $1 / ( 1 - \lambda )$ . Then for $\mathbf z ( \mathrm t ) = \mathbf { \sigma } \mathbf { \sigma } ^ { 2 } ( \mathrm t )$ and $\mathbf { y ( t ) } = \varepsilon ^ { 2 } ( \mathrm { t } )$ , equation (5.3.11) is equivalent to equation (5.3.7) with $\omega = 0$ 

The GARCH models discussed so far are symmetric in that the shock sign does not affect the resulting volatility. In practice, however, negative price shocks influence volatility more than the positive shocks. A noted example of the asymmetric GARCH model is the exponential GARCH (EGARCH) (see, e.g., [3]). It has the form 

$$
\begin{array}{c} \log [ \sigma^ {2} (t) ] = \omega + \beta \log [ \sigma^ {2} (t - 1) ] + \lambda z (t - 1) + \\ \gamma (| z (t - 1) | - \sqrt {2 / \pi}) \end{array}\tag{5.3.12}
$$

where $\mathbf { \boldsymbol { z } } ( \mathrm { t } ) = \varepsilon ( \mathrm { t } ) / \mathbf { \sigma } \mathbf { \boldsymbol { \sigma } } ( \mathrm { t } )$ . Note that $\operatorname { E } [ z ( \operatorname { t } ) ] = { \sqrt { 2 / \pi } }$ . Hence, the last term in (5.3.12) is the mean deviation of z(t). If $\gamma > 0$ and $\lambda < 0$ , negative shocks lead to higher volatility than positive shocks. 

## 5.4 MULTIVARIATE TIME SERIES

Often the current value of a variable depends not only on its past values, but also on past and/or current values of other variables. Modeling of dynamic interdependent variables is conducted with multivariate time series. The multivariate models yield not only new implementation problems but also some methodological difficulties. In particular, one should be cautious with simple regression models 

$$
\mathrm{y} (t) = \mathrm{ax} (t) + \varepsilon (t)\tag{5.4.1}
$$

that may lead to spurious results. It is said that (5.4.1) is a simultaneous equation as both explanatory (x) and dependent (y) variables are present at the same moment of time. A notorious example for spurious inference is the finding that the best predictor in the United Nations database for the Standard & Poor’s 500 stock index is production of butter in Bangladesh [5]. 

A statistically sound yet spurious relationship is named data snooping. It may appear when the data being the subject of research are used to construct the test statistics [4]. Another problem with simultaneous equations is that noise can be correlated with the explanatory variable, which leads to inaccurate OLS estimates of the regression coefficients. Several techniques for handling this problem are discussed in [2]. 

A multivariate time series $\begin{array} { r } { \mathbf { \boldsymbol { y } } ( \mathbf { \boldsymbol { t } } ) = ( \mathbf { \boldsymbol { y } } _ { 1 } ( \mathbf { \boldsymbol { t } } ) , \mathbf { \boldsymbol { y } } _ { 2 } ( \mathbf { \boldsymbol { t } } ) , \dots , \mathbf { \boldsymbol { y } } _ { \mathrm { { n } } } ( \mathbf { \boldsymbol { t } } ) ) ^ { \prime } } \end{array}$ is a vector of n processes that have data available for the same moments of time. It is supposed also that all these processes are either stationary or have the same order of integration. In practice, the multivariate moving average models are rarely used due to some restrictions [1]. Therefore, we shall focus on the vector autoregressive model (VAR) that is a simple extension of the univariate AR model to multivariate time series. Consider a bivariate VAR(1) process 

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathrm {y_ {1} (t) = a_ {10} + a_ {11} y_ {1} (t - 1)+ a_ {12} y_ {2} (t - 1)+ \varepsilon_ {1} (t)} \\ \mathrm {y_ {2} (t) = a_ {20} + a_ {21} y_ {1} (t - 1)+ a_ {22} y_ {2} (t - 1)+ \varepsilon_ {2} (t)} \end{array}\tag{5.4.2}
$$

that can be presented in the matrix form 

$$
\mathbf {y} (t) = \mathbf {a} _ {0} + \mathbf {A y} (t - 1) + \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} (t)\tag{5.4.3}
$$

In (5.4.3), $\begin{array} { r } { \mathbf { y } ( \mathbf { t } ) = ( \mathbf { y } _ { 1 } ( \mathbf { t } ) , \mathbf { y } _ { 2 } ( \mathbf { t } ) ) ^ { \prime } , \mathbf { a } _ { 0 } = ( \mathbf { a } _ { 1 0 } , \mathbf { a } _ { 2 0 } ) ^ { \prime } , \mathbf { \theta } \in ( \mathbf { t } ) = ( \mathbf { \mathbf { \bar { t } } } _ { 1 } ( \mathbf { t } ) , \mathbf { \theta } \mathbf { \bar { \xi } } _ { \mathbf { z } } ( \mathbf { t } ) ) ^ { \prime } , } \end{array}$ and $\mathbf { A } = { \left[ \begin{array} { l l } { \mathbf { a } _ { 1 1 } } & { a _ { 1 2 } } \\ { a _ { 2 1 } } & { a _ { 2 2 } } \end{array} \right] }$ 

The right-hand sides in example (5.4.2) depend on past values only. However, dependencies on current values can also be included (socalled simultaneous dynamic model [1]). Consider the modification of the bivariate process (5.4.2) 

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathrm {y_ {1} (t) = a_ {11} y_ {1} (t - 1)+ a_ {12} y_ {2} (t)+ \varepsilon_ {1} (t)} \\ \mathrm {y_ {2} (t) = a_ {21} y_ {1} (t)+ a_ {22} y_ {2} (t - 1)+ \varepsilon_ {2} (t)} \end{array}\tag{5.4.4}
$$

The matrix form of this process is 

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{c c} 1 & - a _ {1 2} \\ - a _ {2 1} & 1 \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{c} y _ {1} (t) \\ y _ {2} (t) \end{array} \right] = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} a _ {1 1} & 0 \\ 0 & a _ {2 2} \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{c} y _ {1} (t - 1) \\ y _ {2} (t - 1) \end{array} \right] + \left[ \begin{array}{c} \varepsilon_ {1} (t) \\ \varepsilon_ {2} (t) \end{array} \right]\tag{5.4.5}
$$

Multiplying both sides of (5.4.5) with the inverse of the left-hand matrix yields 

$$
\begin{array}{c} \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathrm{y} _ {1} (\mathrm{t}) \\ \mathrm{y} _ {2} (\mathrm{t}) \end{array} \right] = (1 - \mathrm{a} _ {1 2} \mathrm{a} _ {2 1}) ^ {- 1} \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \mathrm{a} _ {1 1} & \mathrm{a} _ {1 2} \mathrm{a} _ {2 2} \\ \mathrm{a} _ {1 1} \mathrm{a} _ {2 1} & \mathrm{a} _ {2 2} \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathrm{y} _ {1} (\mathrm{t} - 1) \\ \mathrm{y} _ {2} (\mathrm{t} - 1) \end{array} \right] \\ + (1 - \mathrm{a} _ {1 2} \mathrm{a} _ {2 1}) ^ {- 1} \left[ \begin{array}{c c} 1 & \mathrm{a} _ {1 2} \\ \mathrm{a} _ {2 1} & 1 \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{c} \varepsilon_ {1} (\mathrm{t}) \\ \varepsilon_ {2} (\mathrm{t}) \end{array} \right] \end{array}\tag{5.4.6}
$$

Equation (5.4.6) shows that the simultaneous dynamic models can also be represented in the VAR form. 

In the general case of n-variate time series, VAR(p) has the form [2] 

$$
\mathbf {y} (t) = \mathbf {a} _ {0} + \mathbf {A} _ {1} \mathbf {y} (t - 1) + \dots + \mathbf {A} _ {\mathbf {p}} \mathbf {y} (t - p) + \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} (t)\tag{5.4.7}
$$

where y(t), a , and «(t) are n-dimensional vectors and $\mathbf { A _ { i } } ( \mathrm { i } = 1 , \dotsc , \mathfrak { p } )$ are n x n matrices. Generally, the white noises «(t) are mutually independent. Let us introduce 

$$
\bar {\mathbf {A}} _ {\mathrm{p}} (\mathbf {L}) = \mathbf {I} _ {\mathrm{n}} - \mathbf {A _ {1}} \mathbf {L} - \dots - \mathbf {A _ {p}} \mathbf {L} ^ {\mathrm{p}}\tag{5.4.8}
$$

where $\mathrm { I _ { n } }$ is the n-dimensional unit vector. Then equation (5.4.7) can be presented as 

$$
\bar {\mathbf {A}} _ {\mathrm{p}} (\mathrm{L}) \mathbf {y} (t) = \mathbf {a} _ {0} + \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} (t)\tag{5.4.9}
$$

Two covariance-stationary processes $\mathbf { \boldsymbol { x } } ( \mathbf { \boldsymbol { t } } )$ and y(t) are jointly covariance-stationary if their covariance $\mathrm { C o v ( x ( t ) , y ( t - s ) ) }$ depends on lag s only. The condition for the covariance-stationary $\operatorname { V A R } ( { \mathfrak { p } } )$ is the generalization of (5.1.11) for $\operatorname { A R } ( { \mathfrak { p } } )$ . Namely, all values of z satisfying the equation 

$$
| \mathbf {I} _ {\mathrm{n}} - \mathbf {A _ {1}} z - \dots - \mathbf {A _ {p}} z ^ {\mathrm{p}} | = 0\tag{5.4.10}
$$

must lie outside the unit circle. Equivalently, all solutions of the equation 

$$
| \mathbf {I} _ {\mathrm{n}} \lambda^ {\mathrm{p}} - \mathbf {A _ {1}} \lambda^ {\mathrm{p-1}} - \dots - \mathbf {A _ {p}} | = 0\tag{5.4.11}
$$

must satisfy the condition $| \lambda | < 1$ 

The problem of whether the lagged values of process y can improve prediction of process x (so-called Granger causality) is often posed in forecasting. It is said that if y fails to Granger-cause x, then the following condition holds for all $\mathbf s > 0$ 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} \mathrm{MSE} (\mathrm{E} [ \mathrm{x} (t + s) | \mathrm{x} (t), \mathrm{x} (t - 1), \dots ]) & = \\ \mathrm{MSE} (\mathrm{E} [ \mathrm{x} (t + s) | \mathrm{x} (t), \mathrm{x} (t - 1), \dots , \mathrm{y} (t), \mathrm{y} (t - 1), \dots ]) \end{array}\tag{5.4.12}
$$

In this case, y is called exogenous variable with respect to x. For example, $\mathrm { y } _ { 2 } ( \mathrm { t } )$ is exogenous with respect to $\mathrm { y } _ { 1 } ( \mathrm { t } )$ in (5.4.2) if $\mathbf { a } _ { 1 2 } = 0$ General methods for testing the Granger causality are described in [2]. 

The last notion that is introduced in this section is cointegration. Two processes are cointegrated if they both have unit roots (i.e., they both are I(1) ), but some linear combination of these processes is stationary (i.e., is I(0) ). This definition can be extended to an arbitrary number of processes. As an example, consider a bivariate model 

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathrm {y_ {1} (t) = ay_ {2} (t)+ \varepsilon_ {1} (t)} \\ \mathrm {y_ {2} (t) = y_ {2} (t - 1)+ \varepsilon_ {2} (t)} \end{array}\tag{5.4.13}
$$

Both processes $\mathrm { y } _ { 1 } ( \mathrm { t } )$ and $\mathrm { y } _ { 2 } ( \mathrm { t } )$ are random walks. However the process 

$$
\mathrm{z} (\mathrm{t}) = \mathrm{y} _ {1} (\mathrm{t}) - \mathrm{ay} _ {2} (\mathrm{t})\tag{5.4.14}
$$

is stationary. Details of testing the integration hypothesis are described in [2]. Implications of cointegration in financial data analysis are discussed in [3]. 

## 5.5 REFERENCES FOR FURTHER READING AND ECONOMETRIC SOFTWARE

A good concise introduction into the time series analysis is given by Franses [1]. The comprehensive presentation of the subject can be found in monographs by Hamilton [2] and Green [6]. Important specifics of time series analysis in finance, particularly for analysis and forecasting of volatility, are discussed by Alexander in [3]. In this chapter, only time series on homogenous grids were considered. Specifics of analysis of tick-by-tick data on non-homogenous grids are discussed in [7]. It should be noted that the exercises with the econometric software packages are very helpful for learning the subject. Besides the generic scientific software such as SAS, Splus, and Matlab that have modules for the time series analysis, several econometric software packages are available: PCGive, RATS, Shazam, and TSP. While these packages may have the trial and student versions, Easy-Reg offered by H. J. Bierens<sup>5</sup> has sufficient capability for an introductory course and is free of charge. 

## 5.6 EXERCISES

1. Verify equations (5.1.25)–(5.1.27). 

2. Verify if the process $\mathrm { y } ( \mathrm { t } ) = 1 . 2 \mathrm { y } ( \mathrm { t } - 1 ) - 0 . 3 2 \mathrm { y } ( \mathrm { t } - 2 ) + \varepsilon ( \mathrm { t } )$ (where e(t) is IID) is covariance-stationary. 

3. Estimate the linear dividend growth rate from the dividends paid in the last years (verify these data on the AMEX website: http://www.amex.com): 2000 – $1.51, 2001 – $1.42, 2002 – $1.50, and 2003 – $1.63. 

4. Verify equation (5.4.6) for the processes (5.4.4). 

This page intentionally left blank 

Chapter 6 

## Fractals

In short, fractals are the geometric objects that are constructed by repeating geometric patterns at a smaller and smaller scale. The fractal theory is a beautiful theory that describes beautiful objects. Development of the fractal theory and its financial applications has been greatly influenced by Mandelbrot [1]. In this chapter, a short introduction to the fractal theory relevant to financial applications is given. In Section 6.1, the basic definitions of the fractal theory are provided. Section 6.2 is devoted to the concept of multifractals that has been receiving a lot of attention in the recent research of the financial time series. 

## 6.1 BASIC DEFINITIONS

Self-similarity is the defining property of fractals. This property implies that the geometric patterns are isotropic, meaning shape transformations along all coordinate axes are the same. If the geometric patterns are not isotropic, say the object is contracted along the y-axis with a scale different from that of along the x-axis, it is said that the object is self-affine. The difference between self-similarity and self-affinity is obvious for geometric objects. However, only selfaffinity is relevant for the graphs of financial time series [1]. Indeed, since time and prices are measured with different units, their scaling factors cannot be compared. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-11-17/6bce6541-8c3d-4db5-b1d8-4bdc9ce1043d/575693a2b4b8461003fb66467f285b513252d895a06b73fcc6557d727a898127.jpg)



Figure 6.1 Deterministic (a) and stochastic (b) fractals with the same fractal dimension D ¼ ln(5)/ln(3).


If the geometric pattern used in fractal design is deterministic, the resulting object is named a deterministic fractal. Consider an example in path (a) of Figure 6.1 where a square is repeatedly divided into nine small squares and four of them that have even numbers are deleted (the squares are numerated along rows). If four squares are deleted at random, one obtains a randomfractal (one of such fractals is depicted in path (b) of Figure 6.1). While the deterministic and stochastic fractals in Figure 6.1 look quite different, they have the same fractal dimension. Let us outline the physical sense of this notion. 

Consider a jagged line, such as a coastline. It is embedded into a plane. Thus, its dimension is lower than two. Yet, the more zigzagged the line is, the greater part of plane it covers. One may then expect that the dimension of a coastline is higher than one and it depends on a measure of jaggedness. Another widely used example is a crumpled paper ball. It is embedded in three-dimensional space. Yet, the volume of a paper ball depends on the sizes of its creases. Therefore, its dimension is expected to be in the range of two to three. Thus, we come to the notion of the fractal (non-integer) dimension for objects that cannot be accurately described within the framework of Eucli dian geometry. 

There are several technical definitions for the fractal dimension [2]. The most popular one is the box-counting dimension. It implies mapping the grid boxes of size h (e.g., squares and cubes for the twodimensional and the three-dimensional spaces, respectively) onto the object of interest. The number of boxes that fill the object is $\mathrm { N } ( \mathrm { h } ) \sim \mathrm { h } ^ { \mathrm { - D } }$ . The fractal dimension D is then the limit 

$$
\mathrm{D} = \lim _ {\mathrm{h} \rightarrow 0} [ \ln \mathrm{N(h)} / \ln (1 / \mathrm{h}) ]\tag{6.1.1}
$$

The box-counting dimension has another equivalent definition with the fixed unit size of the grid box and varying object size L 

$$
\mathrm{D} = \lim _ {\mathrm{L} \rightarrow \infty} [ \ln \mathrm{N(L)} / \ln (\mathrm{L}) ]\tag{6.1.2}
$$

The fractal dimension for both deterministic and stochastic fractals in Figure 6.1 equals $D = \ln { ( 5 ) } / \ln { ( 3 ) } \approx 1 . 4 6 5$ . Random fractals exhibit self-similarity only in a statistical sense. Therefore, the scale invariance is a more appropriate concept for random fractals than selfsimilarity. 

The iterated function systems are commonly used for generating fractals. The two-dimensional iterated function algorithm for N fixed points can be presented as 

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathrm {X(k + 1) = \rho X(k) + (1- \rho) X _ {F} (i)} \\ \mathrm {Y(k + 1) = \rho Y(k) + (1- \rho) Y _ {F} (i)} \end{array}\tag{6.1.3}
$$

In (6.1.3), r is the scaling parameter; $\mathrm { X } _ { \mathrm { F } } ( \mathrm { i } )$ and $\mathrm { Y } _ { \mathrm { F } } ( \mathrm { i } )$ are the coordinates of the fixed point ${ \mathrm { i } ; \mathrm { i } = 1 , 2 , . . . \mathrm { N } }$ . The fixed point i is selected at every iteration at random. A famous example with $\Nu = 3$ , the Sierpinski triangle, is shown in Figure 6.2. 

Now, let us turn to the random processes relevant to financial time series. If a random process X(t) is self-affine, then it satisfies the scaling rule 

$$
\mathrm{X} (\mathrm{ct}) = \mathrm{c} ^ {\mathrm{H}} \mathrm{X} (\mathrm{t})\tag{6.1.4}
$$

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-11-17/6bce6541-8c3d-4db5-b1d8-4bdc9ce1043d/4693ec07a72802fdbf99686683eb155c220821fc809dbfd6efb639388f7d4d1e.jpg)



Figure 6.2 The Sierpinski triangle with $\rho = 0 . 5$


The parameter H is named the Hurst exponent. Let us introduce the fractional Brownian motion $\mathbf { B } _ { \mathrm { H } } ( \mathrm { t } )$ . This random process satisfies the following conditions for all t and T [1] 

$$
\operatorname{E} [ \mathbf {B} _ {\mathrm{H}} (t + T) - \mathbf {B} _ {\mathrm{H}} (t) ] = 0,\tag{6.1.5}
$$

$$
\mathrm{E} [ \mathrm{B} _ {\mathrm{H}} (\mathrm{t} + \mathrm{T}) - \mathrm{B} _ {\mathrm{H}} (\mathrm{t}) ] ^ {2} = \mathrm{T} ^ {2 \mathrm{H}}\tag{6.1.6}
$$

When $\mathrm { ~ H ~ } = \mathrm { ~ } ^ { 1 } / _ { 2 }$ , the fractional Brownian motion is reduced to the regular Brownian motion. For the Brownian motion, the correlation between the past average $\mathrm { E } [ \mathbf { B } _ { \mathrm { H } } ( \mathrm { t } ) - \mathbf { B } _ { \mathrm { H } } ( \mathrm { t } - \mathrm { T } ) ] / \mathrm { T }$ and the future aver age $\mathrm { E } [ \mathbf { B } _ { \mathrm { H } } ( \mathrm { t + T } ) - \mathbf { B } _ { \mathrm { H } } ( \mathrm { t } ) ] / \mathrm { T }$ equals 

$$
\mathrm{C} = 2 ^ {2 \mathrm{H} - 1} - 1\tag{6.1.7}
$$

Obviously, this correlation does not depend on T. If $\% < \mathrm { H } < 1$ , then $\mathbf { C } > 0$ and it is said that $\mathbf { B } _ { \mathrm { H } } ( \mathrm { t } )$ is a persistent process. Namely, if $\mathbf { B } _ { \mathrm { H } } ( \mathrm { t } )$ grew in the past, it will most likely grow in the immediate future. 

Conversely, if $\mathbf { B } _ { \mathrm { H } } ( \mathrm { t } )$ decreased in the past, it will most probably continue to fall. Thus, persistent processes maintain trend. In the opposite case $( 0 < \mathrm { H } < { ^ { 1 } } / _ { 2 } , \mathrm { C } < 0 )$ , the process is named anti-persistent. It is said also that anti-persistent processes are mean reverting; for example, if the current process innovation is positive, then the next one will most likely be negative, and vice versa. There is a simple relationship between the box-counting fractal dimension and the Hurst exponent 

$$
\mathbf {D} = 2 - \mathbf {H}\tag{6.1.8}
$$

The fractal dimension of a time series can be estimated using the Hurst’s rescaled range (R/S) analysis [1, 3]. Consider the data set $\mathrm { x _ { i } ( i = 1 , \dots { N } ) }$ with mean m and the standard deviation $\mathbf { \sigma } _ { \mathbf { 0 } \mathbf { N } }$ . To define the rescaled range, the partial sums $\mathbf { S } _ { \mathrm { k } }$ must be calculated 

$$
\mathrm{S} _ {\mathrm{k}} = \sum_ {\mathrm{i} = 1} ^ {\mathrm{k}} \left(\mathrm{x} _ {\mathrm{i}} - \mathrm{m} _ {\mathrm{N}}\right), 1 \leq \mathrm{k} \leq \mathrm{N}\tag{6.1.9}
$$

The rescaled range equals 

$$
\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{S} = [ \max (\mathrm{S} _ {\mathrm{k}}) - \min (\mathrm{S} _ {\mathrm{k}}) ] / \sigma_ {\mathrm{N}}, 1 \leq \mathrm{k} \leq \mathrm{N}\tag{6.1.10}
$$

The value of R/S is always greater than zero since max $\mathrm { ( S _ { k } ) > 0 }$ and min $( \mathbf { S } _ { \mathbf { k } } ) < 0$ . For given R/S, the Hurst exponent can be estimated using the relation 

$$
\mathrm{R} / \mathrm{S} = (\mathrm{aN}) ^ {\mathrm{H}}\tag{6.1.11}
$$

where a is a constant. The R/S analysis is superior to many other methods of determining long-range dependencies. But this approach has a noted shortcoming, namely, high sensitivity to the short-range memory [4]. 

## 6.2 MULTIFRACTALS

Let us turn to the generic notion of multifractals (see, e.g., [5]). Consider the map filled with a set of boxes that are used in the boxcounting fractal dimension. What matters for the fractal concept is whether the given box belongs to fractal. The basic idea behind the notion of multifractals is that every box is assigned a measure m that characterizes some probability density (e.g., intensity of color between the white and black limits). The so-called multiplicative process (or cascade) defines the rule according to which measure is fragmented when the object is partitioned into smaller components. The fragmentation ratios that are used in this process are named multipliers. The multifractal measure is characterized with the Ho¨lder exponent a 

$$
\alpha = \lim _ {h \rightarrow 0} [ \ln \mu (h) / \ln (h) ]\tag{6.2.1}
$$

where h is the box size. Let us denote the number of boxes with given h and a via $\mathrm { N } _ { \mathrm { h } } ( \alpha )$ . The distribution of the Ho¨lder exponents in the limit h $ 0$ is sometimes called the multifractal spectrum 

$$
\mathrm{f} (\alpha) = - \lim _ {\mathrm{h} \rightarrow 0} [ \ln \mathrm{N} _ {\mathrm{h}} (\alpha) / \ln (\mathrm{h}) ]\tag{6.2.2}
$$

The distribution f(a) can be treated as a generalization of the fractal dimension for the multifractal processes. 

Let us describe the simplest multifractal, namely the binomial measure m on the interval [0, 1] (see [5] for details). In the binomial cascade, two positive multipliers, m and $\mathfrak { m } _ { 1 }$ , are chosen so that $\mathbf { m } _ { 0 } + \mathbf { m } _ { 1 } = 1$ . At the step ${ \mathrm { k } } = 0$ , the uniform probability measure for mass distribution, $\mu _ { 0 } = 1$ , is used. At the next step $( \mathrm { k } = 1 )$ , the measure $\mu _ { 1 }$ uniformly spreads mass in proportion $\mathrm { m } _ { 0 } / \mathrm { m } _ { \mathrm { l } }$ on the intervals $[ 0 , \mathbf { \alpha } ^ { 1 } / \mathbf { \beta } _ { 2 } ]$ and $[ ^ { 1 } / _ { 2 } , 1 ]$ , respectively. Thus, $\mu _ { 1 } \lbrack 0 , \ \% \ v { r } = \mathrm { m } _ { 0 }$ and $\mu _ { 1 } [ { \bf \Phi } ^ { 1 } / _ { 2 } { \bf \Phi } , 1 ] = \mathbf { m } _ { 1 }$ . In the next steps, every interval is again divided into two subintervals and the mass of the interval is distributed between subintervals in proportion $\mathrm { m } _ { 0 } / \mathrm { m } _ { \mathrm { l } }$ . For example, at $\begin{array} { r } { \mathbf { k } = 2 { : } \mu _ { 2 } [ 0 , { ^ { 1 } } / _ { 4 } ] } \end{array}$ $\mathbf { \Lambda } = \mathfrak { m } _ { 0 } \mathfrak { m } _ { 0 } , \sharp _ { 2 } [ \mathbf { \Lambda } ^ { 1 } / _ { 4 } , \mathbf { \Lambda } ^ { 1 } / _ { 2 } ] = \sharp _ { 2 } [ ^ { 1 } / _ { 2 } , \mathbf { \Lambda } ^ { 3 } / _ { 4 } ] = \mathfrak { m } _ { 0 } \mathfrak { m } _ { 1 } , \mathfrak { k } _ { 2 } [ ^ { 3 } / _ { 4 } , 1 ] = \mathfrak { m } _ { 1 } \mathfrak { m } _ { 1 }$ and so on. At the ${ \mathrm { k } } ^ { \mathrm { t h } }$ iteration, mass is partitioned into $2 ^ { \mathrm { k } }$ intervals of length $2 ^ { - \mathrm { k } }$ Let us introduce the notion of the binary expansion $0 . \beta _ { 1 } \beta _ { 2 } \ldots \beta _ { \mathrm { k } }$ for the point $\mathbf { x } = \mathsf { \beta } _ { 1 } 2 ^ { - 1 } + \mathsf { \beta } _ { 2 } 2 ^ { - 2 } + \mathsf { \beta } _ { \mathrm { k } } 2 ^ { - \mathrm { k } }$ where $0 \leq \mathbf { x } \leq 1$ and $0 < \beta _ { \mathrm { k } } < 1$ . Then the measure for every dyadic interval $\mathrm { I _ { 0 \beta 1 \beta 2 \dots \beta k } }$ of length $2 ^ { - \mathrm { k } }$ equals 

$$
\mu_ {0 \beta 1 \beta 2 \dots \beta k} = \prod_ {i = 1} ^ {k} m _ {\beta_ {i}} = m _ {0} ^ {n} m _ {1} ^ {k - n}\tag{6.2.3}
$$

where n is the number of digits 0 in the address $0 { \dot { \beta } } _ { 1 } { \beta } _ { 2 } \dots { \beta } _ { \mathrm { k } }$ of the interval’s left end, and $( \mathbf { k } - \mathbf { n } )$ is the number of digits 1. Since the subinterval mass is preserved at every step, the cascade is called conservative or microcanonical. The first five steps of the binomial cascade with ${ \mathrm { m } } _ { 0 } = 0 . 6$ are depicted in Figure 6.3. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-11-17/6bce6541-8c3d-4db5-b1d8-4bdc9ce1043d/2f060aee7b17e7597990e5166c5a181d29dd0e5e89bc7e4017c16573444570dd.jpg)


![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-11-17/6bce6541-8c3d-4db5-b1d8-4bdc9ce1043d/7a5e5b9570e885a42e10b866a03ad63328db2423a0ef2b90750f05de8a43c2c0.jpg)


![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-11-17/6bce6541-8c3d-4db5-b1d8-4bdc9ce1043d/80ac29fc31a3e80095660dee8af1ee04b0252aa8ace94607dd3b6026c67ff3d8.jpg)


![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-11-17/6bce6541-8c3d-4db5-b1d8-4bdc9ce1043d/a5a9c88cad7adcc4217ac38c1395b6fcc2701845f51a1c92e1f044541e5f9baf.jpg)


![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-11-17/6bce6541-8c3d-4db5-b1d8-4bdc9ce1043d/d596ca5b8749a7879847f7db472b59a75f582459fbbac0416c595fcd55cecd32.jpg)


![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-11-17/6bce6541-8c3d-4db5-b1d8-4bdc9ce1043d/f165e4d0f5e5bf844fd7dd0bf9954dcd3080710ce211e4c8e091872818f76779.jpg)



Figure 6.3 Binomial cascade with $\begin{array} { r } { \mathbf { m } _ { 0 } = 0 . 6 \colon \mathrm { a } ) } \end{array}$ ) k ¼ 0, b) k ¼ 1, c) k ¼ 2, d) $\mathrm { k } = 3 , \mathrm { e } ) \mathrm { k } = 4 , \mathrm { f } ) \mathrm { k } = 5$


The multifractal spectrum of the binomial cascade equals 

$$
\mathrm{f} (\alpha) = - \frac {\alpha_ {\max} - \alpha}{\alpha_ {\max} - \alpha_ {\min}} \log_ {2} \left(\frac {\alpha_ {\max} - \alpha}{\alpha_ {\max} - \alpha_ {\min}}\right) - \frac {\alpha - \alpha_ {\min}}{\alpha_ {\max} - \alpha_ {\min}} \log_ {2} \left(\frac {\alpha - \alpha_ {\min}}{\alpha_ {\max} - \alpha_ {\min}}\right)\tag{6.2.4}
$$

The distribution (6.2.4) is confined with the interval $[ \mathsf { d } _ { \mathrm { m i n } } , \mathsf { \alpha } \mathsf { \alpha } _ { \mathrm { m a x } } ]$ . If $\mathrm { m } _ { 0 } \geq 0 . 5$ , then $\mathsf { \alpha } \alpha _ { \mathrm { m i n } } = - \log _ { 2 } \left( \mathsf { m } _ { 0 } \right)$ and $\alpha _ { \mathrm { m a x } } = - \log _ { 2 } { ( 1 - \mathfrak { m } _ { 0 } ) }$ . The binomial cascade can be generalized in two directions. First, one can introduce a multinomial cascade by increasing the number of subintervals to $\mathbf N > 2$ . Note that the condition 

$$
\sum_ {0} ^ {\mathrm{N-1}} \mathrm {m_ {i}} = 1\tag{6.2.5}
$$

is needed for preserving the conservative character of the cascade. Secondly, the values of m can be randomized rather than assigned fixed values. A cascade with randomized $\mathrm { m } _ { \mathrm { i } }$ is called canonical. In this case, the condition (6.2.5) is satisfied only on average, that is 

$$
\mathrm{E} \left[ \sum_ {0} ^ {\mathrm{N} - 1} \mathrm{m} _ {\mathrm{i}} \right] = 1\tag{6.2.6}
$$

An example of the randomized cascade that has an explicit expression for the multifractal spectrum is the lognormal cascade [6]. In this process, the multiplier that distributes the mass of the interval, M, is determined with the lognormal distribution $( \mathrm { i } . \mathrm { e } . , \mathrm { l o g } _ { 2 } ( \mathbf { M } )$ is drawn from the Gaussian distribution). If the Gaussian mean and variance are $\lambda$ and $\mathbf { \delta } _ { \mathbf { { 0 } } , }$ respectively, then the conservative character of the cascade $\mathrm { E } [ \mathbf { M } ] = 0 . 5$ is preserved when 

$$
\sigma^ {2} = 2 (\lambda - 1) / \ln (2)\tag{6.2.7}
$$

The multifractal spectrum of the lognormal cascade that satisfies (6.2.7) equals 

$$
\mathrm{f} (\alpha) = 1 - \frac {(\alpha - \lambda) ^ {2}}{4 (\lambda - 1)}\tag{6.2.8}
$$

Note that in contrast to the binomial cascade, the lognormal cascade may yield negative values of $\mathrm { f } ( { \boldsymbol { \mathsf { \alpha } } } )$ , which requires interpretation of $\operatorname { f } ( \alpha )$ other than the fractal dimension. 

Innovation of multifractal process, $\Delta \mathbf { X } = \mathbf { X } ( \mathrm { t } + \Delta \mathrm { t } ) - \mathbf { X } ( \mathrm { t } )$ , is described with the scaling rule 

$$
\mathrm{E} [ | (\Delta \mathrm{X}) | ^ {\mathrm{q}} ] = \mathrm{c} (\mathrm{q}) (\Delta \mathrm{t}) ^ {\tau (\mathrm{q}) + 1}\tag{6.2.9}
$$

where $\mathsf { c } ( \mathsf { q } )$ and $\tau ( \mathbf { q } )$ (so-called scaling function) are deterministic functions of q. It can be shown that the scaling function $\tau ( \mathrm { q } )$ is always concave. Obviously, $\tau ( 0 ) = - 1$ . A self-affine process (6.1.4) can be treated as a multifractal process with $\tau ( \mathrm { q } ) = \mathrm { H q } - 1$ . In particular, for the Wiener processes, $\bar { \mathrm { H } } = \%$ and $\tau _ { \mathrm { w } } ( \mathrm { q } ) = \mathrm { q } / 2 - 1$ . The scaling function of the binomial cascade can be expressed in terms of its multipliers 

$$
\tau (\mathrm{q}) = \log_ {2} \left(\mathrm{m} _ {0} ^ {\mathrm{q}} + \mathrm{m} _ {1} ^ {\mathrm{q}}\right)\tag{6.2.10}
$$

The scaling function $\tau ( \mathbf { q } )$ is related to the multifractal spectrum f(a) via the Legendre transformation 

$$
\tau (\mathrm{q}) = \min _ {\alpha} [ \mathrm{q} \alpha - \mathrm{f} (\alpha) ]\tag{6.2.11}
$$

which is equivalent to 

$$
\mathrm{f} (\alpha) = \arg \min _ {\mathrm{q}} [ \mathrm{q} \alpha - \tau (\mathrm{q}) ]\tag{6.2.12}
$$

Note that $\mathrm { f } ( \alpha ) = \boldsymbol { \mathrm { q } } ( \alpha - \mathrm { H } ) + 1$ for the self-affine processes. 

In practice, the scaling function of a multifractal process X(t) can be calculated using so-called partition function 

$$
\mathrm{S} _ {\mathrm{q}} (\mathrm{T}, \Delta t) = \sum_ {\mathrm{i} = 0} ^ {\mathrm{N} - 1} | \mathrm{X} (t + \Delta t) - \mathrm{X} (t) | ^ {\mathrm{q}}\tag{6.2.13}
$$

where the sample X(t) has N points within the interval [0, T] with the mesh size $\Delta \mathrm { t } .$ . It follows from (6.2.9) that 

$$
\log \left\{\mathrm{E} \left[ \mathrm{S} _ {\mathrm{q}} (\mathrm{T}, \Delta t) \right] \right\} = \tau (\mathrm{q}) \log (\Delta t) + \mathrm{c} (\mathrm{q}) \log \mathrm{T}\tag{6.2.14}
$$

Thus, plotting log $\{ \mathrm { E } [ \mathrm { S } _ { \mathrm { q } } ( \mathrm { T } , \Delta \mathfrak { t } ) ] \}$ against log (Dt) for different values of q reveals the character of the scaling function $\tau ( \mathbf { q } )$ . Multifractal models have become very popular in analysis of the financial time series. We shall return to this topic in Section 8.2 

## 6.3 REFERENCES FOR FURTHER READING

The Mandelbrot’s work on scaling in the financial time series is compiled in the collection [1]. Among many excellent books on fractals, we choose [2] for its comprehensive material that includes a description of relations between chaos and fractals and an important chapter on multifractals [5].
